Generalized multicategories: change-of-base, embedding, and descent
Rui Prezado, Fernando Lucatelli Nunes

Abstract
. Via the adjunction − ⋅ 1 ⊣ 𝒱 ( 1 , − ) : 𝖲𝗉𝖺𝗇 ( 𝒱 ) → 𝒱 - 𝖬𝖺𝗍 -\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\dashv\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\mathsf{Span}(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat} and a cartesian monad T T on an extensive category 𝒱 \mathcal{V} with finite limits, we construct an adjunction − ⋅ 1 ⊣ 𝒱 ( 1 , − ) : 𝖢𝖺𝗍 ( T , 𝒱 ) → ( T ¯ , 𝒱 ) - 𝖢𝖺𝗍 -\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\dashv\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\mathsf{Cat}(T,\mathcal{V})\to(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat} between categories of generalized enriched multicategories and generalized internal multicategories, provided the monad T T satisfies a suitable condition, which is satisfied by several examples. We verify, moreover, the left adjoint is fully faithful, and preserves pullbacks, provided that the copower functor − ⋅ 1 : 𝖲𝖾𝗍 → 𝒱 -\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V} is fully faithful. We also apply this result to study descent theory of generalized enriched multicategorical structures. These results are built upon the study of base-change for generalized multicategories, which, in turn, was carried out in the context of categories of horizontal lax algebras arising out of a monad in a suitable 2-category of pseudodouble categories.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
18B10,18B15,18B50,18D65,18N10,18N15

Introduction

The systematic study of the dichotomy between enriched categories and internal
categories can be traced as far back as
[
44
, Section 2.2]
. It was
shown in
[
35
, Theorem 9.10]
that for a suitable base category
\mathcal{V}
, the category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
of enriched
\mathcal{V}
-categories can be
fully embedded into the category
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathcal{V})
of categories internal to
\mathcal{V}
, enabling us to view enriched
\mathcal{V}
-categories as

discrete
categories internal to
\mathcal{V}
1
1

1

This embedding
result was studied in more detail in
[
13
]
.
. This observation is, for
example, employed in the study of descent theory of enriched categories (see

[
35
, Theorem 9.11]
and
[
37
]
). The aim of this work is to
construct such an embedding in the setting of generalized multicategories,
which we recall below.

Multicategories, defined in
[
31
, p. 103]
, are structures that
generalize categories, by allowing the domains of morphisms to consist of a
finite list of objects. The most quintessential example is the multicategory

\mathsf{Vect}
, whose objects are vector spaces, and whose morphisms are
multilinear maps. Their “multicomposition” and the description of the
analogous notions of associativity and identity can succinctly be described
via the free monoid monad on
\mathsf{Set}
. More precisely, multicategories can
be formalized by considering the equipment
\Span(\mathsf{Set})
of spans in
\mathsf{Set}
(see
[
4
, p. 22]
), and extending the free monoid monad to a
suitable monad
(-)^{*}
on
\Span(\mathsf{Set})
(see
[
22
, Corollary
A.4]
).

Generalized multicategories
have since been developed in various
contexts, abstracting the notion of ordinary multicategories by replacing the
monad
(-)^{*}
on
\Span(\mathsf{Set})
by a
suitable notion of monad
on a pseudodouble category
.

Enriched
T
-categories
were first introduced in
[
12
]
with
the terminology
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories
. In this setting, the
category of
(T,\mathcal{V})
-categories is obtained out of the so-called

lax extension
of a monad on
\mathsf{Set}
to a suitable monad on
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
, the ubiquitous equipment of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices (see

[
12
, Section 2]
). For instance, when
\mathcal{V}
is a suitable
quantale, the ultrafilter monad
\mathfrak{U}
on
\mathsf{Set}
admits a lax
extension
\overline{\mathfrak{U}}
to
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}

[
12
, Section 8]
.
In particular, when
\mathcal{V}=2
, we have an equivalence
\mathsf{Top}\simeq(\overline{\mathfrak{U}},2)\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
(first observed in
[
3
]
) and

(\overline{\mathfrak{U}},[0,\infty])\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
is equivalent to the
category of approach spaces.

Internal
T
-categories
were introduced in
[
8
]
and

[
22
]
. For a category
\mathcal{B}
with pullbacks, the former defines

T
-categories for any monad
T
on
\mathcal{B}
, while the latter
considers
T
to be a
cartesian
monad on
\mathcal{B}
. A cartesian
monad
T
on
\mathcal{B}
induces a strong monad on the equipment
\Span(\mathcal{B})
of spans in
\mathcal{B}
. In this setting, we can obtain the category
\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{B})
of
T
-categories internal to
\mathcal{B}
. As examples, we
recover the category of ordinary multicategories by considering
\mathsf{Cat}((-)^{*}.\mathsf{Set})
, and letting
\mathfrak{F}
be the free category monad on
\mathsf{Grph}
, we obtain the category
\mathsf{VDbCat}=\mathsf{Cat}(\mathfrak{F},\mathsf{Grph})
of virtual
double categories.

The main goal of this paper is to construct an embedding
(\overline{T},\,\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{V})
from a category
\mathcal{V}
, and a monad
T
on
\mathcal{V}
,
satisfying suitable properties. To this end, it is desirable to work in a
general setting where these various notions of generalized multicategories can
be uniformly studied and compared with one another. This was, in part,
accomplished by the work of
[
15
]
, where the notion of

T
-monoids
was introduced, unifying the several approaches to the
theory of generalized multicategories. To be precise, these
T
-monoids are
the
horizontal lax algebras
induced by a monad
T=(\mathbb{E},T,e,m)
in the 2-category
\mathsf{VDbCat}
of virtual double categories, lax
functors and vertical transformations. These objects have a natural structure
of a virtual double category, which we denote here by
\LaxTHAlg
.

This general setting ought to provide us an “internalization” functor
(\overline{T},\,\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{V})
obtained from the comparison
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\Span(\mathcal{V})
, and
the induced monad
T
on
\Span(\mathcal{V})
. However,
[
15
]
does not provide
a notion of
change-of-base
induced by an appropriate notion of
morphism
S\to T
of monads, where
S=(\mathbb{D},S,e,m)
is
another monad in
\mathsf{VDbCat}
. We remark this was left as future work in

[
15
, 4.4]
.

It should be noted that the study of change-of-base functors has been studied
in each specific setting of generalized multicategories. In
[
33
, Section
6.7]
, the author provides such constructions for the internal case, and

[
12
, Sections 5, 6]
treats two particular families of monad morphisms
for the enriched case. To establish a relationship between the enriched and
internal structures, we expand on the work of these authors, with the goal of
providing a convenient environment to produce and study such a functor
(\overline{T},\,\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{V})
from simpler tools.

We must mention that our approach diverges from the techniques and tools
developed in
[
15
]
. Firstly, we must restrict our scope from virtual
double categories to pseudodouble categories, as we need to work with

(op)lax horizontal transformations
, which require horizontal
composition to be defined. Secondly, instead of using (op)cartesian 2-cells
and their universal properties, we opted to use a “mate theory” of conjoints
and companions to prove our results, mostly to obtain explicit formulas.
Lastly, this is far from the full scope of the project mentioned in

[
15
, 4.4]
, as we merely study the underlying categories and functors
of the 2-dimensional structures formed by these horizontal lax algebras.
Instead, we fall back on an
ad-hoc
approach for the natural
transformations between the functors induced by monad (op)lax morphisms,
leaving a treatment of the complete story for future work.

Outline of the paper:

We begin by reviewing the notion of
pseudodouble category
in Section

1
, first introduced in
[
20
]
, and the two dimensional
structures formed by these, in Section
1
. For pseudodouble
categories
\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}
, the structures consisting of

–

lax functors

\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
as 0-cells,

–

vertical transformations
as vertical
2
2

2

In accordance with

[
15
]
, we take the vertical arrows to be non-strict ones instead.

1-cells,

–

(op)lax horizontal transformations
as horizontal 1-cells,

–

generalized modifications
as 2-cells.

are, by Proposition
1.8
, pseudodouble categories
\Lax_{\mathsf{lax}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
(
\Lax_{\mathsf{opl}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
). We
also have a third double category
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
(Proposition

1.4
) consisting of

–

pseudodouble categories
as 0-cells,

–

(op)lax
functors as (vertical) horizontal 1-cells,

–

generalized vertical transformations
as 2-cells.

The pseudodouble categories that concern our study are the following:

–

the pseudodobule category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices for
suitable monoidal categories
\mathcal{V}
,

–

the pseudodouble category
\Span(\mathcal{B})
of spans in
\mathcal{B}
, for
categories
\mathcal{B}
with pullbacks,

–

the double category of lax
T
-algebras, for
T
a pseudomonad on a
2-category
\mathbb{B}
.

We will furthermore review the double categorical structure of the last item.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a distributive category with finite limits. Section

2
is devoted to studying the pseudodouble categories
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
and
\Span(\mathcal{V})
, and the (op)lax functors induced by the adjunction
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\dashv\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathsf{Set}
. We confirm these functors
induce
\mathsf{Cat}
-graph morphisms

-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\Span(\mathcal{V})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\Span(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat},

which give us an adjunction
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\dashv\mathcal{V}(1,-)
in the 2-category

\mathsf{Grph}(\mathsf{Cat})
(Lemma
2.1
). We also prove that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\Span(\mathcal{V})
defines an oplax functor of pseudodouble
categories (Proposition
2.2
). Using techniques from the
following couple of sections, we obtain the following

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Span(\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1{,}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

which is a generalized notion of adjunction – a
conjunction
– in
the double category
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
.

Section
3
aims to recall the notions of “adjoint” in
pseudodouble categories:
conjoints
and
companions
. These
were first introduced in
[
19
]
, under different terminology. We provide
an explicit description of “mate theory” for these objects (also studied in

[
19
,
41
]
), analogous to the mate theory for adjunctions. We also
take the opportunity to work out some known results for three reasons: first,
to fix technical notation for subsequent sections; second, to serve as
examples on their use; and finally, to keep this work self-contained. This
Section culminates in our first contribution, crucial to construct functor
between categories of lax horizontal algebras, Theorem

3.6
. It states that, if
\mathbb{E}
is

conjoint closed
, then so is
\Lax_{\mathsf{lax}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
.

In Section
4
, we explicitly establish an equivalence
(Proposition
4.1
) between the double category
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
and the double category of pseudo-algebras for the free internal

\mathsf{Cat}
-category 2-monad on the 2-category
\mathsf{Grph}
, with the goal of
making the tools of two-dimensional algebra
[
7
,
28
,
34
]

available to the theory of pseudodouble categories. In particular, via
doctrinal adjunction
[
36
, Theorem 1.4.11]
, we conclude that
\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\Span(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
is a lax functor, and is the conjoint of
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\Span(\mathcal{V})
in
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
.

After recalling the notion of horizontal lax algebra from
[
15
]
, in
Section
5
we prove Theorem
5.2
; it
states that any monad lax morphism
(G,\psi)\colon T\to S
in
\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}
induces a
change-of-base
functor
G_{!}\colon\LaxTHAlg\to\LaxSHAlg
, and any monad oplax morphism
(F,\phi)\colon S\to T
satisfying a suitable conditon also induces a change-of-base functor

F_{!}\colon\LaxSHAlg\to\LaxTHAlg
. We close this section by comparing
our constructions with the change-of-base functors for generalized
multicategories considered in
[
33
]
and
[
12
]
.

In Section
6
, we consider a conjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\,\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

in the double category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{lax}})
, and we proceed to the
existence of an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LaxSHAlg}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\LaxTHAlg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

between the induced change-of-base functors; this is Theorem

6.1
. We also study the conditions for invertibility of unit
and counit of such an adjunction, stated in Lemma
6.2

and Corollary
6.4
. Finally, after instanciating these
results to the settings considered both in
[
33
]
and
[
12
,
24
]
, we take the opportunity to point out some of obstacles to the double
pseudofunctoriality of
\LaxHAlg
.

We devote Section
7
to the study of extensive categories.
When
\mathcal{C}
is an lextensive category, we provide a description of
\mathsf{Fam}(\mathcal{C})
via Artin glueing (Lemma
7.1
), from which we deduce
that the coproduct functor
\sum\colon\mathsf{Fam}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}
preserves finite
limits. Studying limits of fibered categories, we obtain Theorem

7.2
: it confirms that, in a lextensive category, the
coproduct of a “pullback-indexed” family of pullback diagrams is itself a
pullback diagram. This result is extensively employed, as illustrated in the
remaining results of this Section as well as subsequent ones.

The final groundwork is laid down in Section
8
. Via a
“structure transfer”-type of result (Proposition
8.1
),
we are able to construct a monad
\overline{T}
on
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
from a monad
T

on
\Span(\mathcal{V})
, which is, in turn, induced by a cartesian monad
T
on a
lextensive category
\mathcal{V}

[
22
]
. In fact, we obtain a conjunction

(0.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{T},\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(T,\Span(\mathcal{V}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1,\,\hat{\varepsilon}_{T(-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{V}(1,-),\,\mathcal{V}(1,T\hat{\varepsilon}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

in the double category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{lax}})
. However, only under a
suitable condition does this induce an adjunction

(0.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{T},\,\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{V}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1{,}-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

The goal of this Section is to study this extra condition. In the case
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
is fully faithful, we obtain Theorem

8.6
, characterizing this condition in terms of a notion
of
fibrewise discreteness
of a monad. Finally, we check that most of
the commonly studied cartesian monads on lextensive categories
\mathcal{V}

are fibrewise discrete, provided
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
is fully
faithful.

Section
9
contains our main results. Let
\mathcal{V}
be a
lextensive category such that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathsf{Set}\to\mathcal{V}
is fully
faithful, and let
T
be a fibrewise discrete, cartesian monad on
\mathcal{V}
. We also denote the induced monad on
\Span(\mathcal{V})
by
T
. Via Theorem

6.1
, we obtain the (ordinary) adjunction
(
0.2
) from the conjunction (
0.1
) in
\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}
(Theorem
9.2
).

We then apply Theorem
9.2
to study effective descent
morphisms for enriched categorical structures in Section
10
.
Under an additional technical condition (satisfied by most of the examples we
provided), we confirm that
(\overline{T},\,\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}
is precisely the full subcategory of

\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{V})
with a
discrete
object-of-objects (Theorem

10.3
), generalizing
[
35
, 9.10 Theorem]
and

[
13
, Corollary 4.5]
. Via this description, we confirm that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon(\overline{T},\,\mathcal{V})\text{-}\mathsf{Cat}\to\mathsf{Cat}(T,\,\mathcal{V})
reflects effective descent morphisms (Lemma

10.4
), and, with the results of
[
38
]
pertaining
to effective descent morphisms in internal categorical structures, we provide
criteria for an enriched
(\overline{T},\mathcal{V})
-functor to be effective for
descent (Theorem
10.5
). We finalize the paper by
studying the above examples.

1.
Structure of double categories

Double categories were first defined in
[
18
]
, and the more general
pseudodouble categories were introduced in
[
20
]
, allowing the vertical
structure to be non-strict. Here, we will recall this notion of pseudodouble
category, following the opposite convention of taking the horizontal structure
to be non-strict, instead of the vertical, as in
[
15
]
.

Furthermore, to fix notation, we also recall the notions of lax functor, and
generalized vertical transformation
[
19
, 2.2]
, and we provide
definitions for (op)lax horizontal transformations, and the corresponding
notion of modifications. For later reference, we also work out the
(pseudo)double categorical structures formed by these objects.

1.1.
Pseudodouble categories:

A pseudodouble category
\mathbb{D}
consists of:

–

A category
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, denoting its objects as
0-cells
,
its morphisms as
vertical 1-cells
, its composition and
identities as
vertical
.

–

A category
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, denoting its objects as
horizontal
1-cells
, its morphisms as
2-cells
, its composition and
identities as
vertical
.

–

Vertical
domain and codomain functors
\dom,\,\cod\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{0}
,

–

A
horizontal unit
functor
1\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
,

–

A
horizontal composition
functor
\cdot\colon\mathbb{D}_{2}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
for each triple
x,y,z
of 0-cells, denoted by

horizontal composition
, where
\mathbb{D}_{2}
, given by
pullback of
\dom
and
\cod
, is the category of

composable pairs
of horizontal 1-cells and 2-cells.

This data must satisfy
\dom\circ 1=\cod\circ 1=\mathsf{id}
, and
\dom(s\cdot r)=\dom(r)
,
\cod(s\cdot r)=\cod(s)
. Furthermore,
we say a natural transformation
\phi\colon F\to G
of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
is
globular
if
\dom\cdot\phi
and

\cod\cdot\phi
are identities. We also have data

–

Globular natural isomorphisms
\lambda\colon 1_{\cod(-)}\cdot-\to-
,
\rho\colon-\cdot 1_{\dom(-)}\to-
of functors
\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
, the
left
and
right unitors
,
respectively.

–

A globular natural isomorphism
\alpha\colon(-_{1}\cdot-_{2})\cdot-_{3}\to-_{1}\cdot(-_{2}\cdot-_{3})
of functors
\mathbb{D}_{3}\to\mathbb{D}_{1}
, the
associator
, where
\mathbb{D}_{3}
is the category of

composable triples
.

These must also satisfy the following
coherence conditions
:

(a)

We have
\gamma_{1_{x}}=\mathsf{id}_{1_{x}\cdot 1_{x}}
, where we define
\gamma=\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda
.

(b)

The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{z}\cdot s)\cdot r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\cdot(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\cdot r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{r,s,1_{z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{s}\cdot\mathsf{id}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{s\cdot r}}

for each pair of horizontal 1-cells
r\colon x\to y
,
s\colon y\to z
.

(c)

The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\cdot r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\cdot r)\cdot 1_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\cdot(r\cdot 1_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1_{x},r,s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{s}\cdot\rho_{r}}

for each pair of horizontal 1-cells
r\colon x\to y
,
s\colon y\to z
.

(d)

The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\cdot 1_{y})\cdot r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\cdot(1_{y}\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\cdot r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{r,1_{y},s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{s}\cdot\mathsf{id}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{s}\cdot\lambda_{r}}

for each pair of horizontal 1-cells
r\colon x\to y
,
s\colon y\to z
.

(e)

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((t\cdot s)\cdot r)\cdot q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t\cdot s)\cdot(r\cdot q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\cdot(s\cdot(r\cdot q))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t\cdot(s\cdot r))\cdot q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\cdot((s\cdot r)\cdot q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q,r,t\cdot s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{r,s,t}\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{r\cdot q,s,t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{q,s\cdot r,t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{t}\cdot\alpha_{q,r,s}}

for each quadruple of composable horizontal 1-cells
q,r,s,t
.

We will usually supress the subscripts, unless the need to disambiguate
occurs. If
\lambda,\rho
and
\alpha
are the identity
transformations, we say
\mathbb{D}
is a
double category
.

Proposition 1.1
.

The coherence conditions
(a)
,
(b)

and
(c)
are redundant.

Proof.

First, observe that
(b)
is the horizontal dual of

(c)
, so it is sufficient to verify

(a)
and
(b)
.

We may obtain
(a)
from the remaining conditions: we
have an equality of 2-cells
(1_{x}\cdot 1_{x})\cdot 1_{x}\to 1_{x}

\lambda\circ(\lambda\cdot 1)=\lambda\circ\lambda\circ\alpha=\lambda\circ(1\cdot\lambda)\circ\alpha=\lambda\circ(\rho\cdot 1)

by
(b)
, naturality of
\lambda
, and

(d)
. We deduce that
\lambda\cdot 1=\rho\cdot 1
, and since
\rho
is a natural isomorphism, we conclude that
\lambda=\rho
.

To prove
(b)
given only
(d)
and

(e)
, we consider the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{z}\cdot(1_{z}\cdot(s\cdot r))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{z}\cdot(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{z}\cdot((1\cdot s)\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{z}\cdot s)\cdot r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{z}\cdot 1_{z})\cdot(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{z}\cdot(1_{z}\cdot s))\cdot r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((1_{z}\cdot 1_{z})\cdot s)\cdot r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot(\lambda\cdot\mathsf{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}\cdot\lambda)\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\rho\cdot\mathsf{id})\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Except for the top left triangle, every inner polygon commutes either
by
(d)
or by naturality of
\alpha
. The outer
pentagon is an instance of
(e)
, so we conclude that
the top left triangle commutes. Since
\lambda
is a natural isomorphism,
the result follows.
∎

1.2.
Lax functors:

Let
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{E}
be double categories. A lax functor
F\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
consists of:

–

A functor
F_{0}\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{0}
.

–

A functor
F_{1}\colon\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}
.

–

A globular natural transformation
\unit^{F}\colon 1\cdot F_{0}\to F_{1}\cdot 1
.

–

A globular natural transformation
\mult^{F}\colon F_{1}(-_{1})\cdot F_{1}(-_{2})\to F_{1}(-_{1}\cdot-_{2})
.

This data must satisfy the following properties:

–

\dom\circ F_{1}=F_{0}\circ\dom

–

\cod\circ F_{1}=F_{0}\circ\cod
,

–

Comparison coherences for the unit: the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Fy}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F1_{y}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(1_{y}\cdot r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{F}\cdot Fr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\lambda^{-1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr\cdot F1_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(r\cdot 1_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\rho^{-1}}

–

Comparison coherence for composition: the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ft\cdot Fs)\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t\cdot s)\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F((t\cdot s)\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ft\cdot(Fs\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ft\cdot F(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(t\cdot(s\cdot r))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}

Dually, an
oplax functor

F\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{C}
is the
horizontally dual notion (reverse the 2-cells). If the unit comparison
transformation is an isomorphism, we say
F
is
normal
, and if both
comparisons are isomorphisms, then we say
F
is a
strong functor

(which can be seen both as a lax and oplax functor).

Proposition 1.2
.

Composition of (op)lax functors is well-defined, associative, and has
identities. That is to say,
\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{lax}}
(
\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{opl}}
) with
double categories as objects and (op)lax functors as morphisms forms a
category.

Proof.

For a double category
\mathbb{D}
, the identity functor is given by the
identity function on objects and identity functor hom-categories, with
coherence morphisms given by identities. The coherence conditions
trivialize, thus we get a strong functor.

For lax functors
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
and
G\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
, define the
GF
to be given by

–

(GF)_{0}=G_{0}F_{0}
,

–

(GF)_{1}=G_{1}F_{1}
,

–

\unit^{GF}=G\unit^{F}\circ\unit^{G}
,

–

\mult^{GF}=G\mult^{F}\circ\mult^{G}
.

To verify
GF
is a lax functor, first note that
\unit^{GF}
and
\mult^{GF}
are natural transformations, and globular, since
G
is a lax
functor. Next, observe that the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{G}\cdot GFf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\unit^{F}\cdot GFf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\unit^{F}\cdot Fr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mult^{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\lambda^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF\lambda^{-1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFf\cdot G\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFf\cdot\unit^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mult^{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(Fr\cdot\unit^{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\rho^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF\rho^{-1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(\mult^{F}\cdot Fr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mult^{F}\cdot GFr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}\cdot GFr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFt\cdot\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFt\cdot G\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(Ft\cdot\mult^{F})}

commute, since every inner polygon commutes: either by coherence (of both

F
and
G
) or by naturality (only in
\mult^{G}
). Hence, the morphisms
on the boundaries are equal, which give the coherences for
GF
. If
\unit^{F},\,\mult^{F},\,\unit^{G},\,\mult^{G}
are isomorphisms, then so are
\unit^{GF}
and

\mult^{GF}
.

Finally, note that the identity functors are the units for lax functor
composition, and this operation is also associative. This is because all
required compositions occur on categories: function composition on a
category of sets, functor composition on
\mathsf{Cat}
, and 2-cell composition
on the hom-categories (plus the composition preservation by the functors
between them).
∎

1.3.
Vertical transformations

We fix lax functors
H\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
,
K\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
and oplax functors
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{C}
and
G\colon\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{D}
. A
generalized vertical
transformation

\phi
, depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

so that the vertical domain, codomain are
F
,
G
respectively, and
horizontal domain, codomain given by
H
,
K
respectively.

–

A natural transformation
\phi_{0}\colon G_{0}H_{0}\to K_{0}F_{0}
,

–

A natural transformation
\phi_{1}\colon G_{1}H_{1}\to K_{1}F_{1}
,

satisfying
\dom\cdot\phi_{1}=\phi_{0}\cdot\dom
and
\cod\cdot\phi_{1}=\phi_{0}\cdot\cod
, subject to the following conditions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{GHx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{KFx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G1_{Hx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GH1_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KF1_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\phi_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{K}_{Fx}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{G}_{Hx}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\unit^{H}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\unit^{F}_{x}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GHs\cdot GHr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KFs\cdot KFr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(Hs\cdot Hr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(Fs\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GH(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KF(s\cdot r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{s}\cdot\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{K}_{Fs,Fr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}_{Hs,Hr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mult^{H}_{s,r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\mult^{F}_{s,r}}

We say
\phi
is a vertical transformation between lax functors if
F=\mathsf{id}
and
G=\mathsf{id}
, in which case we denote it simply as
\phi\colon H\to K
, and vertical transformations between oplax functors can be
analogously defined.

Proposition 1.3
.

Lax functors and (generalized) vertical transformations form a category, and
the vertical domain, codomain operations define functors to
\mathsf{PsDbCat}_{\mathsf{opl}}
.

Proof.

The identity vertical transformation on a lax functor
F
is given by

\mathsf{id}_{F_{0}}
and
\mathsf{id}_{F_{1}}
, which trivially satisifies the
conditions, and has identities functors as vertical domain and codomain.

To define composition, we consider the generalized vertical transformations

\phi
and
\psi
as given below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

We define
(\psi\circ\phi)_{i}=\psi_{i}F_{i}\circ K_{i}\phi_{i}
for
i=0,1
. Since we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\phi_{s}\cdot K\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Fs}\cdot\psi_{Fr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\phi_{s}\cdot\phi_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\mult^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG\mult^{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Fs\cdot Fr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\mult^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\phi_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KQ\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{F(s\cdot r)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RH\mult^{F}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{K\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\psi_{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(1_{\phi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\unit^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\unit^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KG\unit^{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1_{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\unit^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces KQ\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{F1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RH\unit^{F}}

we conclude that
\psi\circ\phi
is a generalized vertical
transformation, with vertical domain
HF
and codomain
KG
. Hence, if
\phi
and
\psi
are globular, then so is
\psi\circ\phi
, so we obtain a subcategory of lax functors and vertical
transformations.

Associativity and identity are obtained via componentwise calculation on the
underlying natural transformations.
∎

Proposition 1.4

(
[
19
, 2.2]
)
.

We have a double category
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
with double categories, lax and
oplax functors and generalized vertical transformations as 0-cells,
horizontal and vertical 1-cells and 2-cells, respectively.

Proof.

The underlying
\mathsf{Cat}
-graph for this double category is described in
Proposition
1.3
.

For an oplax functor
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{D}
, the identity
natural transformation on
F
defines a generalized vertical
transformation
1_{F}\colon 1_{\mathsf{id}}\to 1_{\mathsf{id}}
, whose vertical domain
and codomain is
F
.

Given generalized vertical transformations
\phi
and
\psi
as
given by the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

we denote their horizontal composite by
\psi\cdot\phi
, and is
defined by
(\psi\cdot\phi)_{i}=S_{i}\phi_{i}\circ\psi_{i}P_{i}
. Since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Ps}\cdot\psi_{Pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\phi_{s}\cdot S\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\mult^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(\phi_{s}\cdot\phi_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\mult^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{Ps\cdot Pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HR\mult^{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\mult^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SG\mult^{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{P(s\cdot r)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\phi_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SQ\mult^{F}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\psi P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{S\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\unit^{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S1_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\unit^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1_{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HR\unit^{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\unit^{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SG\unit^{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{P1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces SQ\unit^{F}}

are commutative diagrams, we conclude
\psi\cdot\phi
is a generalized
vertical transformation, with vertical domain
F
and codomain
H
.
Associativity and identity conditions hold, via componentwise calculation on
the underlying natural transformations, so we may take the associator and
unitor isomorphisms to be identites.
∎

1.4.
Horizontal transformations:

Let
F,\,G\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
be lax functors. A
lax
horizontal transformation

\phi\colon F\to G
is given by data

–

a functor
\phi\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}

–

a globular natural transformation
\nat^{\phi}\colon G\cdot\phi_{\dom}\to\phi_{\cod}\cdot F
of functors
\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}
.

satisfying the following coherence conditions:

–

Comparison coherence for the unit: the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Gx}\cdot\phi_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{x}\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G1_{x}\cdot\phi_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{x}\cdot F1_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{G}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{1_{x}}}

for all 0-cells
x
.

–

Comparison coherence for the composition: the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(s\cdot r)\cdot\phi_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{z}\cdot F(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Gs\cdot Gr)\cdot\phi_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{z}\cdot(Fs\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gs\cdot(Gr\cdot\phi_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi_{z}\cdot Fs)\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gs\cdot(\phi_{y}\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Gs\cdot\phi_{y})\cdot Fr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\phi}_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{G}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\phi}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\phi}_{s}\cdot\mathsf{id}}

for each pair
r\colon x\to y
,
s\colon y\to z
of horizontal
1-cells.

Oplax horizontal transformations
between (op)lax functors are
obtained by dualizing 2-cells, and
strong horizontal transformations

are those whose comparison 2-cells are invertible, and hence are
simultaneously lax and oplax.

Proposition 1.5
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
be a lax functor. The data

(i)

(1_{F})_{x}=1_{Fx}
for each 0-cell
x
,

(ii)

(1_{F})_{f}=1_{Ff}
for each vertical 1-cell
f
,

(iii)

\nat^{1_{F}}_{r}=\gamma_{Fr}
for each horizontal 1-cell
r
,

defines a strong horizontal transformation
1_{F}\colon F\to F
.

Proof.

The data
(i)
and
(ii)
tell us that the
underlying functor of
1_{F}
is the composite
1\circ F_{0}\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}
, and the datum
(iii)
tells us
that the underlying globular natural transformation
\nat^{1_{F}}
is given
by the whiskering
\gamma\cdot F_{1}\colon F\cdot 1_{\dom}\to 1_{\cod}\cdot F
, so the data is well-defined.

We’re left with checking coherence. First, we observe that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Fx}\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Fx}\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Fx}\cdot F1_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F1_{x}\cdot 1_{Fx}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

commutes by naturality, giving the comparison coherence diagram for the
unit. Now, note that we have

(1.1)

\alpha^{-1}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\gamma)\circ\alpha\circ(\gamma\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}=\gamma,

by
(b)
,
(d)
and

(c)
, so that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Fz}\cdot F(s\cdot r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(s\cdot r)\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Fz}\cdot(Fs\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Fs\cdot Fr)\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{Fz}\cdot Fs)\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fs\cdot(Fr\cdot 1_{Fx})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Fs\cdot 1_{Fy})\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fs\cdot(1_{Fy}\cdot Fr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\gamma}

by naturality of
\gamma
. Since
\gamma\cdot F_{1}
is invertible,
we conclude that
1_{F}\colon F\to F
is a strong horizontal
transformation.
∎

Proposition 1.6
.

Let
\phi\colon F\to G
,
\psi\colon G\to H
be lax horizontal
transformations, where
F,G,H\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
are lax
functors. The data

(i)

(\psi\cdot\phi)_{x}=\psi_{x}\cdot\phi_{x}\colon Fx\to Hx
for
each 0-cell
x
,

(ii)

(\psi\cdot\phi)_{f}=\psi_{f}\cdot\phi_{f}
for each vertical 1-cell

f
,

(iii)

\nat^{\psi\cdot\phi}_{r}=\alpha^{-1}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\phi}_{r})\circ\alpha\circ(\nat^{\psi}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}
for each
horizontal 1-cell
r

defines a lax horizontal transformation
\psi\cdot\phi\colon F\to H
.

Proof.

Due to functoriality of horizontal composition and of the underlying
functors of
\phi
and
\psi
, it is enough to point out that
\phi_{x}
) and
\psi_{x}
are a composable pair of horizontal 1-cells to
make sure the data
(i)
and
(ii)
define
a functor
\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}
.

Furthermore, note that the datum
(iii)
is a composite of
globular natural transformations, so it is enough to verify the coherence
conditions are satisfied.

We note the following diagram, in which we have supressed the horizontal
1-cells,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{H}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\unit^{H}\cdot\mathsf{id})\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{G})\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot(\unit^{G}\cdot\mathsf{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot(\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\psi}_{1}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\phi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}

commutes, by naturality of
\alpha
and unit comparison coherence for
\psi
and
\phi
. By
1.1
, the top composite is
\gamma
, so this confirms unit comparsion coherence for
\psi\cdot\phi
.

The next diagram verifies composition coherence for
\psi\cdot\phi
:
it is a pasting of composition coherences for
\psi
and
\phi
, a
naturality square from the functoriality of
\cdot
,
and the remaining diagrams are coherence and naturality of
\alpha
.

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\phi}_{s\cdot r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha^{-1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{id}}

Hence, we have confirmed composition coherence for

,
