∎

1.5.
Modifications

Let
F,\,G,\,H,\,K\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
be lax functors and let

\zeta\colon F\to H
,
\xi\colon G\to K
be oplax horizontal
transformations, and let
\phi\colon F\to G
,
\psi\colon H\to K

be vertical transformations. A
modification

\Gamma\colon\zeta\to\xi
, depicted as

(1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}

is a natural transformation
\Gamma\colon\zeta\to\xi
on the
underlying functors
\zeta,\xi\colon\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}

such that

(1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{y}\cdot Gr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hr\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kr\cdot\xi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{y}\cdot\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\xi}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{r}\cdot\Gamma_{x}}

commutes for all horizontal 1-cells
r\colon x\to y
. We say
\phi

and
\psi
are respectively the vertical domain and codomain of
\Gamma
.

Proposition 1.7
.

We have a category
\Lax_{\mathsf{opl}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
with oplax horizontal
transformations of lax functors
\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
as objects, and
modifications as morphisms. Moreover, the vertical domain and codomain
operations define functors to the category of lax functors and vertical
transformations.

Proof.

Let
\zeta\colon F\to G
be an oplax transformation of lax functors
\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
. We take the identity modification
\mathsf{id}_{\zeta}

on
\zeta
to be given by identity natural transformation on the
underlying functor of
\zeta
, whose vertical domain and codomain are
taken to be the identity vertical transformations
\mathsf{id}_{F}
and
\mathsf{id}_{G}
, respectively. The instance of the diagram (
1.3
) for
\mathsf{id}_{\zeta}
is trivially commutative.

Let
\Gamma,\Xi
be modifications given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}

We define the composite
\Gamma\circ\Xi
to be the vertical
composition of the underlying natural transformations. Since

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{y}\cdot Hr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{y}\cdot Lr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gr\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kr\cdot\xi_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Mr\cdot\chi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{y}\cdot\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\xi}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{y}\cdot\theta_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\chi}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{r}\cdot\Gamma_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{r}\cdot\Xi_{x}}

commutes for all horizontal 1-cells
r\colon x\to y
, we confirm
\Xi\circ\Gamma\colon\phi\to\chi
is a modification with vertical
domain
\theta\circ\phi
and codomain
\omega\circ\psi
.

Associativity and identity properties are inherited from natural
transformations, and functoriality of vertical domain and codomain is an
immediate consequence.
∎

Proposition 1.8
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{E}
be double categories.
\Lax_{\mathsf{opl}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
has the structure of a double category, with lax functors as
0-cells, vertical transformations as vertical 1-cells, oplax horizontal
transformations as horizontal 1-cells, and modifications as 2-cells.

Proof.

The underlying categories of cells are provided in Propositions

1.2
and
1.7
. moreover, the latter
has provided the vertical domain and codomain functors.

We have defined the horizontal unit functor on objects in Proposition

1.5
. For a vertical transformation
\phi\colon F\to G
,
we define
1_{\phi}
to be the modification with underlying natural
transformation
1\cdot\phi_{0}
, with vertical domain
1_{F}
and
codomain
1_{G}
; note that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{F}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{G}\cdot Gr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr\cdot 1_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gr\cdot 1_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\phi}\cdot\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{r}\cdot 1_{\phi}}

commutes by naturality of
\gamma
. Since this is just whiskering with

1\colon\mathbb{E}_{0}\to E_{1}
, this describes a functor.

We have defined the horizontal composition functor on objects in Proposition

1.6
. For modifications
\Gamma
and
\Xi
as depicted
below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}

we define
\Xi\cdot\Gamma
to be the horizontal composition of the
underlying natural transformations. This is a modification, since the
following diagram commutes

(1.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi_{y}\cdot\zeta_{y})\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{y}\cdot(\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{y}\cdot(Hr\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega_{y}\cdot\theta_{y})\cdot Gr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{y}\cdot(\theta_{y}\cdot Gr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{y}\cdot(Kr\cdot\theta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi_{y}\cdot Hr)\cdot\zeta x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Lr\cdot\xi_{x})\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Lr\cdot(\xi_{x}\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\omega_{y}\cdot Kr)\cdot\theta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Mr\cdot\omega_{x})\cdot\theta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Mr\cdot(\omega_{x}\cdot\theta_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Xi_{y}\cdot\Gamma_{y})\cdot\phi_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot n^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{y}\cdot(\Gamma_{y}\cdot\phi_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi_{y}\cdot(\psi_{r}\cdot\Gamma_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot n^{\theta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\xi}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Xi_{y}\cdot\psi_{r})\cdot\Gamma_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\chi_{r}\cdot\Xi_{x})\cdot\Gamma_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{r}\cdot(\Xi_{x}\cdot\Gamma_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{\omega}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

and has vertical domain
\phi
and codomain
\chi
.

Since horizontal composition in
\mathbb{E}
is functorial, we obtain
functoriality of horizontal composition of modifications. Moreover, both the
horizontal unit and horizontal composition have the required behaviour with
respect to vertical domains and codomains.

We’re left with providing the unitors and associator, and the respective
proofs that these satisfy the required coherence conditions.
Moreover, we define
\lambda_{\zeta}\colon 1_{H}\cdot\zeta\to\zeta
to
be given by
\lambda_{\zeta_{x}}\colon 1_{Hx}\cdot\zeta_{x}\to\zeta_{x}
,
and
\rho_{\zeta}
is similarly defined. These are globular modifications,
as the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{Hy}\cdot\zeta_{y})\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Hy}\cdot(\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{Hy}\cdot(Hr\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{Hy}\cdot Hr)\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Hr\cdot 1_{Hx})\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hr\cdot(1_{Hx}\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hr\cdot\zeta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\zeta_{y}}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\lambda_{\zeta_{x}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\zeta_{y}\cdot 1_{Fy})\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{y}\cdot(1_{Fy}\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{y}\cdot(Fr\cdot 1_{Fx})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\zeta_{y}\cdot Fr)\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Hr\cdot\zeta_{x})\cdot 1_{Fx}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hr\cdot(\zeta_{x}\cdot 1_{Fx})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hr\cdot\zeta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\zeta_{y}}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\zeta}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\rho_{\zeta_{x}}}

by naturality of
\lambda,\rho
, and coherence.

Finally, we let
\pi\colon L\to P
be another oplax horizontal
transformation. We define
\alpha\colon(\pi\cdot\xi)\cdot\zeta\to\pi\cdot(\xi\cdot\zeta)
to be given at
x
by
\alpha\colon(\pi_{x}\cdot\xi_{x})\cdot\zeta_{x}\to\pi_{x}\cdot(\xi_{x}\cdot\zeta_{x})
.
Also a natural isomorphism, and is a globular modification since the
following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\pi_{y}\cdot\xi_{y})\cdot\zeta_{y})\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{y}\cdot(\xi_{y}\cdot\zeta_{y}))\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{y}\cdot\xi_{y})\cdot(\zeta\cdot Fr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{y}\cdot((\xi_{y}\cdot\zeta_{y})\cdot Hr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{y}\cdot\xi_{y})\cdot(Hr\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{y}\cdot(\xi_{y}\cdot(\zeta_{y}\cdot Hr))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\pi_{y}\cdot\xi_{y})\cdot Hr)\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{y}\cdot(\xi\cdot(Hr\cdot\zeta_{x}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{y}\cdot(\xi_{y}\cdot Hr))\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{y}\cdot((\xi_{y}\cdot Hr)\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{y}\cdot(Lr\cdot\xi_{x}))\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{y}\cdot((Lr\cdot\xi_{x})\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\pi_{y}\cdot Lr)\cdot\xi_{x})\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{y}\cdot(Lr\cdot(\xi_{x}\cdot\zeta_{x}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((Pr\cdot\pi_{x})\cdot\xi_{x})\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{y}\cdot Lr)\cdot(\xi_{x}\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Pr\cdot(\pi_{x}\cdot\xi_{x}))\cdot\zeta_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Pr\cdot\pi_{x})\cdot(\xi_{x}\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pr\cdot((\pi_{x}\cdot\xi_{x})\cdot\zeta_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Pr\cdot(\pi_{x}\cdot(\xi_{x}\cdot\zeta_{x}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\zeta}_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot(\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\zeta}_{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\xi}_{r})\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot(\nat^{\xi}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\nat^{\pi}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id})\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nat^{\pi}_{r}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\cdot\alpha}

which is obtained by pasting coherence pentagons and naturality squares
of
\alpha
.

By checking componentwise, we find that
\lambda,\rho
and
\alpha
satisfy the desired coherence conditions.
∎

1.6.
Examples:

The pseudodouble categories studied in this body of work are:

–

The pseudodouble category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices, for
distributive monoidal categories
\mathcal{V}
(that is, for
\mathcal{V}

with coproducts, which preserved by the tensor product; see
[
6
,
12
,
15
]
).

–

The pseudodouble category
\Span(\mathcal{B})
of spans of morphsims in
\mathcal{B}
, for
\mathcal{B}
a category with pullbacks (see
[
4
,
22
,
15
]
).

–

The pseudodouble categories
\Lax_{\mathsf{lax}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
and
\Lax_{\mathsf{opl}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
for double categories
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{E}
.

–

The double categories
\LaxTAlg
,
\PsTAlg
of lax and pseudo

T
-algebras, for
T
a pseudomonad on a 2-category
\mathbb{B}
.

–

The double category
\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathbb{B})=\LaxidAlg
of monads in a
2-category
\mathbb{B}
.

We shall specify the double categorical structure of
\LaxTAlg
. First,
recall that we have 2-categories
\LaxTAlg_{\mathsf{lax}}
and
\LaxTAlg_{\mathsf{opl}}

whose 0-cells are lax
T
-algebras, with their (op)lax morphisms and their
respective 2-cells
[
36
]
; however, there is a notion of generalized
2-cell which subsumes both structures.

We will be taking the vertical 1-cells to be the oplax morphisms and the
horizontal 1-cells to be the lax morphisms. Let
(h,\phi)\colon(w,a,\eta,\mu)\to\mathbb{(}x,b,\eta,\mu)
,
(k,\psi)\colon(y,c,\eta,\mu)\to(z,d,\eta,\mu)
be lax
T
-algebra lax morphisms and
(f,\zeta)\colon(w,a,\eta,\mu)\to(y,c,\eta,\mu)
,
(g,\xi)\colon(x,b,\eta,\mu)\to(z,d,\eta,\mu)
be lax
T
-algebra oplax morphisms. A

generalized lax
T
-algebra 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w,a,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,b,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(y,c,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z,d,\eta,\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,\zeta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h,\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k,\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

consists of a 2-cell
\omega\colon g\cdot h\to k\cdot f
satisfying
the following coherence condition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\cdot b\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\cdot h\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\cdot f\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\cdot c\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\cdot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega\cdot a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot\omega^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\cdot\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\cdot Tf}

where we write
\omega^{T}=(\mult^{T})^{-1}\circ T\omega\circ\mult^{T}
.
Horizontal and vertical composition is defined as expected: to be explicit,
we consider generalized lax
T
-algebra 2-cells
\theta,\sigma
given
by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(y,c,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z,d,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x^{\prime},b^{\prime},\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z^{\prime},d^{\prime},\eta,\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k,\xi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,b,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(w^{\prime},a^{\prime},\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(z,d,\eta,\mu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(y^{\prime},c^{\prime},\eta,\mu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g,\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(l,\chi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

and we define
\theta\circ\omega=(\theta\cdot f)\circ(g^{\prime}\cdot\omega)
and
\sigma\cdot\omega=(k^{\prime}\cdot\omega)\circ(\sigma\cdot h)
. These provide a double categorical structure to
\LaxTAlg
,
provided the coherence conditions are satisfied for
\theta\circ\omega

and
\sigma\cdot\omega
, which are given by the commutativity of the
following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot b\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot h\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot k\cdot f\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot Tg^{\prime}\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\cdot f^{\prime}\cdot f\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot k\cdot c\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot Tg^{\prime}\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\cdot f^{\prime}\cdot c\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot Tm\cdot Tf^{\prime}\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\cdot b^{\prime}\cdot Tf^{\prime}\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot\xi\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\omega\cdot a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot\omega^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot k\cdot\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\cdot f\cdot a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}\cdot\psi\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\prime}\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot Tg^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\cdot f^{\prime}\cdot\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta\cdot c\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\cdot\theta^{T}\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\cdot\phi^{\prime}\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\cdot Tf^{\prime}\cdot Tf}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\cdot a^{\prime}\cdot Th^{\prime}\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\cdot h^{\prime}\cdot b\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}\cdot Tl\cdot Th^{\prime}\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\cdot h^{\prime}\cdot h\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot b\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}\cdot Tk^{\prime}\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot h\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}\cdot Tk^{\prime}\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot k\cdot f\cdot a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot k\cdot c\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\cdot\phi^{\prime}\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi\cdot Th^{\prime}\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l\cdot h^{\prime}\cdot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\cdot b\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{T}\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\cdot h\cdot a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot g\cdot\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot\psi\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}\cdot Tg\cdot Th}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}\cdot Tk^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot\omega\cdot a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot\omega^{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{\prime}\cdot Tk\cdot Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot k\cdot\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}\cdot\psi\cdot Tf}

with analogous definitions for
\theta^{T}
and
\sigma^{T}
, plus a
couple of omitted coherence conditions which confirm that
(\theta^{T}\cdot Tf)\circ(Tg^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{T})=(\theta\circ\omega)^{T}
and
(Tk^{\prime}\cdot\omega^{T})\circ(\sigma^{T}\cdot Th)=(\sigma\cdot\omega)^{T}
.

2.
Spans versus matrices

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a distributive, cartesian monoidal category with finite
limits. Our starting point is the adjunction

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Set}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

whose unit and counit we denote by
\hat{\eta},\hat{\varepsilon}
respectively; here,
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
is the copower with the terminal object
1
.

After fixing some notation regarding
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
and
\Span(\mathcal{V})
, we confirm
that (
2.1
) induces an adjunction of internal
\mathsf{Cat}
-graph
morphisms

-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\Span(\mathcal{V})\qquad\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\Span(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}

and we will furthermore confirm that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
defines an oplax functor.

Together with the tools and terminology provided in
Sections
3
and
4
, we will be able to deduce
that
\mathcal{V}(1,-)
is a lax functor, and that we have a conjunction in the
double category
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
. The unit and counit may be depicted as
follows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Span(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\eta}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Span(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Span(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Span(\mathcal{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}}

alluding to the fact that these are generalized vertical transformations in
\mathsf{PsDbCat}
.

Notation for
\Span(\mathcal{V})
:

The
\mathsf{Cat}
-graph
\Span(\mathcal{V})
is succintly defined as
[l\leftarrow m\rightarrow r,\mathcal{V}]\rightrightarrows\mathcal{V}
, whose underlying functors
are the evaluations at
l
and
r
. Throughout this work, we opt to denote
spans
p\colon X\nrightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{V}
as the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{p}}

and a 2-cell
\theta
will be denoted as a morphism
M_{p}\to M_{q}

making both of the following squares commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{q}}

The unit span
1_{X}\colon X\nrightarrow X
is defined on objects by
M_{1_{X}}=X
and
l_{1_{x}}=r_{1_{x}}=\mathsf{id}_{X}
, and on morphisms
f\colon X\to Y
by
l_{f}=r_{f}=f
.

Let
q\colon Y\nrightarrow Z
be another span in
\mathcal{V}
. We write the
pullback which defines
q\cdot p
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q\cdot p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{p}}

so that we have
l_{q\cdot p}=l_{p}\circ\pi_{1}
and
r_{q\cdot p}=r_{q}\circ\pi_{0}
. By abuse of notation, we may refer to instances of such
pullback diagrams as
M_{q\cdot p}
.

The unitors
\lambda\colon 1\cdot p\to p
and
\rho\colon p\cdot 1\to p
in
\Span(\mathcal{V})
are given by the pullback projections
\pi_{1}\colon M_{1\cdot p}\to M_{p}
and
\pi_{0}\colon M_{p\cdot 1}\to M_{p}
,
respectively.

Given a third span
r\colon Z\nrightarrow W
, note that the universal
property of the pullback
M_{q\cdot p}
guarantees the existence of a
unique map
\pi_{2}\colon M_{(r\cdot q)\cdot p}\to M_{q\cdot p}
such
that
\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2}=\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}=\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{2}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{(r\cdot q)\cdot p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{r\cdot q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q\cdot p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{p}}

With this, the associator
\alpha\colon(r\cdot q)\cdot p\to r\cdot(q\cdot p)
may be defined as the unique map such that
\pi_{1}\circ\alpha=\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{0}\circ\alpha=\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{0}
, via the universal
property of the pullback
M_{r\cdot(q\cdot p)}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{(r\cdot q)\cdot p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{r\cdot(q\cdot p)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q\cdot p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{q}\circ\pi_{0}}

Notation for
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
:

Let
p\colon U\nrightarrow V
be a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix. We denote by
p(u,v)\in\mathcal{V}
the value of
p
at the pair
(u,v)\in U\times V
. A
2-cell of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

consists of a family of morphisms
\theta_{u,v}\colon p(u,v)\to q(fu,gv)
in
\mathcal{V}
, for
u\in U
and
v\in V
. Given another 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}

the composite
\omega\circ\theta
is given at
u,v
by the composite
of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(u,v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q(fu,gv)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(hfu,kgv),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{u,v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{fu,gv}}

exhibiting the structure of
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
as an internal
\mathsf{Cat}
-graph.

Given
u,u^{\prime}\in U
, we write
[u=u^{\prime}]
for the set that is a singleton
if
u=u^{\prime}
and empty otherwise. Note that if we have a function
f\colon U\to V
, then there is a unique morphism
[u=u^{\prime}]\to[fu=fu^{\prime}]
. With
this, the unit
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
1_{U}\colon U\nrightarrow U
is defined by

1_{U}(u,u^{\prime})=[u=u^{\prime}]\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
for a set
U
, and
1_{f}
is given by
1_{f}(u,u^{\prime})\colon[u=u^{\prime}]\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to[fu=fu^{\prime}]\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
for a function
f\colon U\to V
.

Recall that if
t\colon V\nrightarrow W
is another
\mathcal{V}
-matrix, we
have

(t\cdot s)(u,w)=\sum_{v\in V}t(v,w)\times s(u,v)

which is the composition of
\mathcal{V}
-matrices. This is likewise defined
for 2-cells.

The unitors and associators are then given by taking coproducts over the
unitors and associators for the cartesian monoidal structure of
\mathcal{V}
.

Lifting the adjunction to
\mathsf{Grph}(\mathsf{Cat})
:

For a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
p\colon X\nrightarrow Y
, we define
M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}=\sum_{x,y}p(x,y)
, and we define
p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\nrightarrow Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
to be the span given by taking the coproduct of
p(x,y)\to 1

indexed by
X\times Y
; this gives a morphism
M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\to X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\times Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
(see Diagram (
2.2
) below, which is
commutative by the universal property of the coproduct), whose composite with
the projections determine
l_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
and
r_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
.

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\times Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\eta}x,\hat{\eta}y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}{,}\,r_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}

We write
\hat{\pi}_{0}\colon(t\cdot s)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
(respectively,
\hat{\pi}_{1}\colon(t\cdot s)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
) for the coproducts of the
projections
t(v,w)\times s(u,v)\to t(v,w)
indexed by
U\times V\times W\to V\times W
(respectively,
t(v,w)\times s(u,v)\to s(u,v)
indexed by
U\times V\times W\to U\times V
).

For a span
p\colon V\nrightarrow W
in
\mathcal{V}
, we define the
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
\mathcal{V}(1,p)\colon\mathcal{V}(1,V)\nrightarrow\mathcal{V}(1,W)
to be given
at
v,w
by the following pullback:

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,p)(v,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v,w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p},r_{p}}

and if we have a 2-cell of spans
\theta
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{q}}

then
\mathcal{V}(1,\theta)
is the 2-cell uniquely determined by pullback as
follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,p)(v,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,q)(fv,gw)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,\theta)(v,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces fv,gw}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q},r_{q}}

We observe that
l_{q}\circ\theta=f\circ l_{p}
and
r_{q}\circ\theta=f\circ r_{p}
.

We extend
\hat{\eta}
,
\hat{\varepsilon}
to
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
and
\Span(\mathcal{V})
: for a
\mathcal{V}
-matrix
p\colon X\nrightarrow Y
, we define
\hat{\eta}_{p}\colon p\to\mathcal{V}(1,p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)
at
x,y
to be given by the dashed arrow

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(\hat{\eta}x,\,\hat{\eta}y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\times Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\eta}x,\,\hat{\eta}y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1},\,r_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}

For a span
p\colon V\nrightarrow W
, we let
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}\colon\mathcal{V}(1,p)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to p
to be given by taking the coproduct of (
2.3
)
indexed by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,V)\times\mathcal{V}(1,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,V)\times\mathcal{V}(1,W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

which yields a commutative square

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\mathcal{V}(1,p)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,V)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\times\mathcal{V}(1,W)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{V},\,\hat{\varepsilon}_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{p},\,r_{p}}

By taking the coproduct of (
2.4
) over the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

we conclude that
\hat{\varepsilon}_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ\hat{\eta}_{p}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1=\mathsf{id}_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
.
Moreover, by considering the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,s)(v,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,\mathcal{V}(1,s)\cdot 1)(\hat{\eta}v,\hat{\eta}w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}(1,s)(v,w)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{\mathcal{V}(1,s)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{s}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}({1,}\hat{\varepsilon}_{s})_{\hat{\eta}v,\hat{\eta}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v,w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\varepsilon}_{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{s},r_{s}}

we conclude
\mathcal{V}(1,\hat{\varepsilon}_{s})\circ\eta_{\mathcal{V}(1,s)}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathcal{V}(1,s)}
. Hence, we have confirmed that

Proposition 2.1
.

We have an adjunction
-\cdot 1\dashv\mathcal{V}(1,-)\colon\Span(\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
in
\mathsf{Grph}(\mathsf{Cat})
.

Coherence for
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
:

Now, we check that
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
is a normal oplax functor: for a set
X
,
consider the pullback diagram

(2.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[x=y\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{x,y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x,\,y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}

so that
\delta_{x,x}=x
and
\delta_{x,y}
is uniquely determined
when
x\neq y
. Now, we consider the image of the diagram
(
2.6
) under
-\cdot 1
, and take its coproduct indexed by:

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

This yields us
\unit^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}_{X}
; since
[x=y]\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\cong 0
for
x\neq y
, we conclude that this 2-cell is invertible.

Moreover, given a function
f\colon X\to Y
, we observe that the
following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[x=y\right]\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[fx=fy\right]\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\cdot 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\cdot 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{f,x,y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\cdot 1}

commutes, as it is the image via
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
of a commutative diagram in

\mathsf{Set}
. Taking the coproduct over (
2.7
) confirms naturality of

\unit^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
.

For
\mathcal{V}
-matrices
p\colon X\nrightarrow Y
,
q\colon Y\nrightarrow Z
,
\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
is depicted in Diagram (
2.8
) below by a
dashed arrow, and is uniquely determined by the universal property of the
pullback square:

(2.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{(p\cdot q)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{(p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\cdot(q\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{q\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\pi}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\pi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{q\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}

We consider the following horizontally composable 2-cells of
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{1}}

We have

\displaystyle\pi_{j}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}_{q_{0},q_{1}}\circ((\zeta_{1}\cdot\zeta_{0})\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{j}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ((\zeta_{1}\cdot\zeta_{0})\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=(\zeta_{j}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ(\pi_{j}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=(\zeta_{j}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ\pi_{j}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}_{p_{0},p_{1}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{j}\circ((\zeta_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\cdot(\zeta_{0}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1))\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}_{p_{0},p_{1}}

for
j=0,1
. We obtain naturality via the universal property of the
pullback
M_{(q_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\cdot(q_{0}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
.

To verify the unit comparison coherence of
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
, we let
p\colon X\nrightarrow Y
be a span in
\mathcal{V}
, and we consider the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum_{x,y,z}1_{Y}(y,z)\times p(x,y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{(1_{Y}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\cdot(p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1_{Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot(p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum_{x,y}p(x,y).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\unit^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

By definition,
\lambda\colon M_{1_{Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot(p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}\to M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
is simply the pullback projection, thus
\lambda\circ(\unit^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})=\pi_{1}
is the pullback projection
M_{(1_{Y}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\cdot(p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}\to M_{p\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
, and therefore
\lambda\circ(\unit^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}=\pi_{1}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}=\hat{\pi}_{1}
by
(
2.8
). But
\hat{\pi}_{1}
itself is the coproduct of
1_{Y}(y,z)\times p(x,y)\to p(x,y)
indexed by the projection
X\times Y\times Z\to Y\times Z
, which is just
\lambda\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
. A similar
argument confirms the right unitor case.

Now, we’re left with verifying the composition comparison coherence of
-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1
. For the remainder of the section, we will denote horizontal
composition simply by concatenation. For an easier understanding of the
calculations, we provide the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((ts)r)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((ts)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1))(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(ts)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(sr)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t(sr))\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)((sr)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)((s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

First, we verify that
\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ(\pi_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)=\pi_{1}\circ\alpha\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}
as 2-cells
((ts)r)\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\to(s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)
. We have

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ\hat{\pi}_{1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\hat{\pi}_{0}\circ\hat{\pi}_{1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\hat{\pi}_{1}\circ\hat{\pi}_{0}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ\pi_{0}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{0}\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\alpha\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ\hat{\pi}_{1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\hat{\pi}_{1}\circ\hat{\pi}_{1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\hat{\pi}_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\alpha\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1},

and then we apply the universal property of the pullback
M_{(s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)}
. With this, we finish our proof: note that

\displaystyle\pi_{0}\circ\alpha\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{0}\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ\pi_{0}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

\displaystyle=(\pi_{0}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ(\pi_{0}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{0}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\circ(\pi_{1}\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)\circ(\alpha\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}\circ\alpha\circ(\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\mult^{-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1}

then we apply the universal property of
M_{(t\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)((s\boldsymbol{\cdot}1)(r\boldsymbol{\cdot}1))}
.
This concludes the proof of

Proposition 2.2
.

-\boldsymbol{\cdot}1\colon\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}\to\Span(\mathcal{V})
is a normal oplax functor.

3.
Conjoints and companions

As introduced in
[
19
]
, and studied in
[
42
,
15
,
16
,
41
]
,
there exist two notions of “adjunction” between vertical and horizontal
1-cells in a pseudodouble category; these were introduced as

orthogonal adjunctions
.

To be precise, let
\mathbb{D}
be a pseudodouble category, and let
f\colon a\to b
be a vertical 1-cell,
r\colon b\nrightarrow a
be a
horizontal 1-cell. Following the terminology from
[
42
,
41
]
, we say
that
r
is the
conjoint
of
f
if there exist 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

such that
\varepsilon\circ\eta=1_{f}
and
\eta\cdot\varepsilon=\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda
. We say
\eta
,
\varepsilon
are the unit,
counit of the conjoint, respectively. Also denote by
companion
the
horizontally dual notion of conjoint; we denote the unit and counit 2-cells
of a companion as
\nu\colon 1\to r
and
\delta\colon r\to 1
,
respectively.

In any pseudodouble category
\mathbb{D}
, the identity vertical 1-cell on
any 0-cell
x
always has both a companion and a conjoint; in both cases, it
is given by the horizontal unit
1_{x}
, with unit and counit given by
\mathsf{id}_{1_{x}}=1_{\mathsf{id}_{x}}
, which trivially satisfies all four conditions.
Unless otherwise specified,
1_{x}
will be our fixed choice of
companion/conjoint for
\mathsf{id}_{x}
.

We say that
\mathbb{D}
is
conjoint (companion) closed
if every
vertical 1-cell of
\mathbb{D}
has a conjoint (companion). For instance,

equipments
may be defined as the pseudodouble categories which are
both conjoint and companion closed (see
[
42
, Theorem A.2]
), of which

\Span(\mathcal{V})
and
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathsf{Mat}
are examples.

Let
T
be a pseudomonad on a 2-category
\mathbb{B}
, and consider the
double category of lax
T
-algebras as described in Section

1
. Our next result, Proposition
3.1
, given
in
[
36
, Theorems 1.4.11 and 1.4.14]
, originally stated for strict

T
-algebras in
[
28
]
, may be used to characterize conjoints and
companions in
\LaxTAlg
. Since this is just a restatement of the results
of
[
36
, Chapter 1]
, we omit the argument.

Proposition 3.1

(Doctrinal adjunction)
.

Let
(f,g,\eta,\varepsilon)
be an adjunction in a 2-category
\mathbb{B}
. There is a bijection between 2-cells
\zeta
making
(f,\zeta)
into an lax
T
-algebra oplax morphism and 2-cells
\xi
making
(g,\xi)
into a lax
T
-algebra lax morphism.

Moreover,
(f,\zeta)
is the conjoint of
(g,\xi)
in
\LaxTAlg
if and only if
\zeta
and
\xi
correspond to each other via the
aforementioned bijection, and
f
has a companion if and only if
\zeta
is invertible; in which case, its companion is
(f,\zeta^{-1})
.

As is the case with ordinary adjunctions in a 2-category, there is also a
notion of
mate theory
for conjoints (and dually, companions), which
we present in Lemma
3.2
. Results along these
lines were already present in
[
19
, 1.6]
, as well as
[
15
, Corollary
7.21]
and
[
41
, Propositions 5.13 and 5.19]
. We have decided to
provide a slightly different statement and proof: our goal is to provide
explicit formulas as an aid for calculations involving conjoints and
companions, abundant in this work.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
(f,f^{*},\eta,\varepsilon)
and
(g,g^{*},\eta,\varepsilon)
be
conjoints, and consider 2-cells

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r\cdot f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}\cdot s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

\xi=\rho\circ\Big(\big((\eta\circ 1_{h})\cdot\zeta\big)\cdot(1_{k}\circ\varepsilon)\Big)\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}
,

(b)

\zeta=\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{s})\circ\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{r}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}
,

(c)

\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{s})\circ\xi=\rho\circ\big(\zeta\cdot(1_{k}\circ\varepsilon)\big)
,

(d)

\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{r}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}=\big((\eta\circ 1_{h})\cdot\zeta\big)\circ\lambda^{-1}

In particular, the sets of 2-cells as given in (
3.1
)
are in pairwise correspondence, explicitly given by the formulas

(a)
and
(b)
. Pairs of such 2-cells are said
to be mates or under mate correspondence.

Proof.

We will prove that (c)
\to_{\text{i}}
(b)
\to_{\text{ii}}
(d)
\to_{\text{iii}}
(a)
\to_{\text{iv}}
(c).

(i)

Since
\varepsilon\circ\eta=1_{f}
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q})\circ\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}

\displaystyle=\rho\circ\big(\zeta\cdot(1\circ\varepsilon)\big)\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}

\displaystyle=\rho\circ\big((\zeta\circ\mathsf{id}_{p})\cdot(1\circ\varepsilon\circ\eta)\big)\circ\rho^{-1}=\zeta

(ii)

Since
\eta\cdot\varepsilon=\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\big((\eta\circ 1)\cdot\zeta\big)\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=\Big((\eta\circ 1)\cdot\big(\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q})\circ\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{q}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1})\big)\Big)\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}_{s}\cdot\lambda)\circ(\eta\cdot(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q}))\circ\big(1\cdot(\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1})\big)\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}_{s}\cdot\lambda)\circ\alpha\circ(\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q})\circ(\lambda\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}\circ\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}

and coherence guarantees

(\mathsf{id}\cdot\lambda)\circ\alpha\circ(\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\lambda\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}=\mathsf{id},

as desired.

(iii)

Since
\eta\cdot\varepsilon=\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\big((\eta\circ 1)\cdot\zeta\big)\cdot(1\circ\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\big(\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda\big)\cdot\varepsilon

\displaystyle=(\xi\cdot 1)\circ((\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot\eta)\cdot\varepsilon)\circ((\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda)\cdot\mathsf{id}_{g})

\displaystyle=(\xi\cdot 1)\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot(\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda))\circ\alpha\circ((\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda)\cdot\mathsf{id}_{g^{*}})

and coherence guarantees

\rho\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ(\mathsf{id}_{p}\cdot(\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda))\circ\alpha\circ((\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda)\cdot\mathsf{id}_{g^{*}})\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}=\mathsf{id},

as desired.

(iv)

Since
\varepsilon\circ\eta=1_{g}
, we have

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q})\circ\xi

\displaystyle=(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q})\circ\rho\circ\big(((\eta\circ 1)\cdot\zeta)\cdot(1\circ\varepsilon)\big)\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=\rho\circ((\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}_{q})\cdot\mathsf{id}_{1})\circ(((\eta\circ 1)\cdot\zeta)\cdot(1\circ\varepsilon))\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=\rho\circ((1\cdot\zeta)\cdot\varepsilon)\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=\rho\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}\circ(\zeta\cdot\varepsilon)

and coherence guarantees

\rho\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ\lambda^{-1}=\lambda^{-1}\circ\rho,

as desired.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

Once more, we consider the pair of 2-cells
\zeta,\xi
given in
(
3.1
). We will consider the following specialized
instances of the mate correspondence:

(i)

For
k=\mathsf{id}
, (c) becomes
(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\xi=\gamma^{-1}\circ(\zeta\cdot\varepsilon)
.

(ii)

For
h=\mathsf{id}
, (d) becomes
\xi\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\eta)=(\eta\cdot\zeta)\circ\gamma^{-1}
.

(iii)

For
s=1
, (c) becomes
\varepsilon\circ\theta=\rho\circ(\zeta\cdot(1_{k}\circ\varepsilon))
, where
\theta=\rho\circ\xi
.

(iv)

For
r=1
, (d) becomes
\theta\circ\eta=((\eta\circ 1_{h})\cdot\zeta)\circ\lambda^{-1}
, where
\theta=\xi\circ\lambda^{-1}
.

(v)

For
f=\mathsf{id}
, both (b) and (c) become
\zeta=\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\theta
, where
\theta=\xi\circ\rho^{-1}
.

(vi)

For
g=\mathsf{id}
, both (b) and (d) become
\zeta=\theta\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}
, where
\theta=\lambda\circ\xi
.

And by combining these, we may obtain simpler forms. For example,

(v)
and
(iii)
(respectively,

(vi)
and
(iv)
)) provide the result that the
counit (unit) of a conjunction is a cartesian (opcartesian) 2-cell in the
sense of
[
42
,
15
]
.

The combination of
(iii)
and
(iv)
is mainly
used under the hypothesis that we have a commutative square
k\circ f=g\circ h
of vertical 1-cells, that is,
\zeta=\mathsf{id}
. In this case,
the unit
1_{g\circ h}=1_{k\circ f}
has two mates; they are said to
be the
mates of the commutative square

k\circ f=g\circ h
,
and are the unique 2-cells
\theta
,
\omega
, respectively
satisfying

\varepsilon\circ\theta=1_{k}\circ\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\theta\circ\eta=\eta\circ 1_{h}

\varepsilon\circ\omega=1_{g}\circ\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\omega\circ\eta=\eta\circ 1_{f}

In practice, we will consider “the” mate of a commutative square
k\circ f=g\circ h
, and we let context determine which mate is being
considered.

We proceed to review well-known
[
15
,
19
,
16
,
42
,
41
]
, yet
fundamental results about companions and conjoints. Our aim is to demonstrate
the applications of their mate theory, while fixing notation to use for later
reference in Sections
5
and

6
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
be a lax functor of conjoint closed
pseudodouble categories, and let
f
is a vertical 1-cell in
\mathbb{D}
.
We denote the mate of
F\eta\circ\unit^{F}
obtained via
(vi)

by
\sigma^{F}_{f}\colon(Ff)^{*}\to F(f^{*})
:

We say that
F

preserves the conjoint
of
f
if
\sigma^{F}_{f}

is an invertible 2-cell; we say
F

preserves conjoints
if
\sigma^{F}_{f}
is invertible for all vertical 1-cells
f
. We can show that:

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
be a lax functor of conjoint closed
pseudodouble categories. The following are equivalent:

(a)

F
preserves conjoints of identities,

(b)

F
preserves all conjoints,

(c)

F
is normal.

Proof.

We begin by showing that any lax functor
F
satisfies the identity
F\varepsilon\circ\sigma^{F}=\unit^{F}\circ\varepsilon
, for we have

F\varepsilon\circ\sigma^{F}\circ\eta=F\varepsilon\circ F\eta\circ\unit^{F}=F1_{f}\circ\unit^{F}=\unit^{F}\circ 1_{Ff}=\unit^{F}\circ\varepsilon\circ\eta,

so the desired equation follows by
(vi)
.

Moreover, whenever
\sigma^{F}_{f}
is invertible, the following relations
hold:

\displaystyle\varepsilon\circ(\sigma^{F})^{-1}\circ F\eta\circ\unit^{F}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\eta=1_{f},

\displaystyle\unit^{F}\circ\varepsilon\circ(\sigma^{F})^{-1}\circ F\eta

\displaystyle=F\varepsilon\circ F\eta=F1_{f}.

Hence, if
\sigma^{F}_{\mathsf{id}}
is invertible for all 0-cells, we conclude that

\unit^{F}
is invertible; this confirms
(a)

\to

(c)
.

Now, if we assume
F
is normal, we let
\chi^{F}
be the unique 2-cell
such that
\varepsilon\circ\chi^{F}=(\unit^{F})^{-1}\circ F\varepsilon
,
obtained via
(v)
. From this, it is clear that
\chi^{F}\circ\sigma^{F}=\mathsf{id}
, since

\varepsilon\circ\chi^{F}\circ\sigma^{F}=(\unit^{F})^{-1}\circ F\varepsilon\circ\sigma^{F}=\varepsilon,

and

\displaystyle\rho^{-1}\circ\sigma^{F}\circ\chi^{F}\circ\lambda

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\chi^{F})

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\eta\cdot\varepsilon)\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\chi^{F})

\displaystyle=(\sigma^{F}\circ\eta)\cdot(\varepsilon\circ\chi^{F})

\displaystyle=(F\eta\circ\unit^{F})\cdot((\unit^{F})^{-1}\circ F\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}\cdot(\unit^{F})^{-1})\circ(F\eta\cdot F\varepsilon)\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\circ F\rho\circ\mult^{F}\circ(F\eta\cdot F\varepsilon)\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\circ F\rho\circ F(\eta\cdot\varepsilon)\circ\mult^{F}\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda

confirms that
\chi^{F}
is the inverse of
\sigma^{F}
. We have shown
that
(c)

\to

(b)
, and of course,

(a)
is a particular case of
(b)
.
∎

For the case of companions, we write
\tau^{F}_{f}\colon(Ff)_{!}\to F(f_{!})

for the mate of
F\nu\circ\unit^{F}
, and we say that
F

preserves
the companion
of
f
if
\tau^{F}
is invertible. The horizontally dual
result states that
F
preserves companions iff
F
is normal. Thus, we
obtain the result that these three notions are equivalent for lax functors
between pseudodouble categories (
[
16
, Proposition 3.8]
).

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
be a lax functor between conjoint
closed pseudodouble categories, and let
r\colon x\nrightarrow y
,
f\colon z\to y
be horizontal, vertical 1-cells respectively. Then the 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Ff)^{*}\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f^{*})\cdot Fr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(f^{*}\cdot r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mult^{F}}

is invertible. In particular,
\mult^{F}\colon F(f^{*})\cdot Fr\to F(f^{*}\cdot r)
is invertible for all such
r,f
if and only if
F
is normal.

Proof.

We claim the inverse
l^{F}
is given by the mate of
F\theta
via

l^{F}=\hbox to112.17pt{\vbox to82.73pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.08325pt\lower-41.08586pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-56.08325pt}{-31.98615pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

Note that
l^{F}
is the mate of
F\theta
, and
\theta
is the
mate of
\mathsf{id}_{f^{*}\cdot r}
, via
(iv)
and

(v)
, respectively. Now, note that

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ l^{F}\circ\mult^{F}\circ(\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\circ F\theta\circ\mult^{F}\circ(\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\circ F\lambda\circ\mult^{F}\circ(F\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\circ F\lambda\circ\mult^{F}\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})=\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle\mult^{F}\circ(\sigma^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ l^{F}

\displaystyle=\mult^{F}\circ(F\eta\cdot F\theta)\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=F(\eta\cdot\theta)\circ\mult^{F}\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\lambda^{-1}=\mathsf{id}

So, the result follows by the mate correspondence.
∎

In a conjoint closed pseudodouble category
\mathbb{D}
, let
f,g
be
composable vertical 1-cells with conjoints
f^{*}
and
g^{*}
, and let
\pi\colon f^{*}\cdot g^{*}\to(g\circ f)^{*}
be the mate of
1_{g}\circ\varepsilon\colon f^{*}\to 1
. Via
(i)
and
(iii)
,
we obtain:

(3.2)

We can also define a 2-cell
(g\circ f)^{*}\to f^{*}\cdot g^{*}
as the mate
of
\eta\circ 1_{f}
, which can be shown to be the inverse of
\pi
,
using a method similar to the proof of Lemma
3.5
; as
this is not needed, we omit the details.

Now that we have fixed the notation we will need for the rest of the paper,
we end the section with Theorem
3.6
, to justify the
utility of conjoints and companions.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}
be pseudodouble categories. If
\mathbb{E}

is conjoint closed, then so is
\Lax_{\mathsf{lax}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
. Dually,
if
\mathbb{E}
is companion closed, then so is
\Lax_{\mathsf{opl}}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
.

Proof.

Fix a vertical transformation
\phi\colon F\to G
where
F,G\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
are lax functors. For each 0-cell
x
, we write

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{x}}

for the 2-cells satisfying
\varepsilon_{x}\circ\eta_{x}=1_{\phi_{x}}
and

\eta_{x}\cdot\varepsilon_{x}=\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda
, so that
\phi^{*}_{x}
is the conjoint of
\phi_{x}
for all
x
.

Define
\phi^{*}_{f}\colon\phi^{*}_{x}\to\phi^{*}_{y}
to be the mate of
1_{Ff}
via
(ii)
, so that
\phi^{*}_{f}\circ\eta_{x}=\eta_{y}\circ 1_{Ff}
and
\varepsilon_{y}\circ\phi^{*}_{f}=1_{Gf}\circ\varepsilon_{x}
. Moreover, note that

\phi_{g}^{*}\circ\phi^{*}_{f}\circ\eta_{x}=\phi^{*}_{g}\circ\eta_{y}\circ 1_{Ff}=\eta_{z}\circ 1_{Gf}\circ 1_{Ff}=\eta_{z}\circ 1_{G(g\circ f)},

so we conclude that
\phi^{*}_{g\circ f}=\phi_{g}^{*}\circ\phi_{f}^{*}
by
mate correspondence. Similarly, we have
\phi^{*}_{\mathsf{id}_{x}}=\mathsf{id}_{\phi_{x}}^{*}
.

Next, we consider the map
r\mapsto Fr\cdot\phi^{*}_{x}
, where
r\colon x\nrightarrow y
is a horizontal 1-cell. It is functorial: for 2-cells

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

we have

F(\theta\circ\chi)\cdot\phi^{*}_{h\circ f}=(F\theta\circ F\chi)\cdot(\phi^{*}_{h}\circ\phi^{*}_{f})=(F\theta\cdot\phi^{*}_{h})\circ(F\chi\cdot\phi^{*}_{f}),

and
F(\mathsf{id})\cdot\phi^{*}_{\mathsf{id}}=\mathsf{id}
, as desired. Analogously,
r\mapsto\phi^{*}_{y}\cdot Gr
is also functorial.

We define
\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r}\colon Fr\cdot\phi^{*}_{x}\to\phi^{*}_{y}\cdot Gf

to be the mate of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fy}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gr}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{r}}

via
(i)
. We claim this data makes
\phi^{*}
into a lax
horizontal transformation
G\nrightarrow F
.

Given a 2-cell
\theta
as in the left diagram of (
3.3
), we
have
\phi_{s}\circ F\theta=G\theta\circ\phi_{r}
, since
\phi
is
a vertical transformation. The following pairs

\displaystyle G\theta\circ\phi_{r}\quad\text{and}\quad\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s}\circ(F\theta\cdot\phi^{*}_{f}),

\displaystyle\phi_{s}\circ F\theta\quad\text{and}\quad(\phi^{*}_{g}\cdot G\theta)\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r}

are mates, so that we have

\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s}\circ(F\theta\cdot\phi^{*}_{f})=(\phi^{*}_{g}\cdot G\theta)\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r},

giving naturality. To confirm this,

\displaystyle\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s}\circ(F\theta\cdot\phi^{*}_{f})

\displaystyle=\rho\circ(\phi_{s}\cdot\varepsilon)\circ\circ(F\theta\cdot\phi^{*}_{f})

\displaystyle=\rho\circ\big((\phi_{s}\circ F\theta)\cdot(\varepsilon\circ\phi^{*}_{f})\big)

\displaystyle=\rho\circ\big((\phi_{s}\circ F\theta)\cdot(1\circ\varepsilon)\big),

\displaystyle(\phi^{*}_{g}\cdot G\theta)\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\phi^{*}_{g}\cdot G\theta)\circ(\eta\cdot\phi_{r})\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big((\phi^{*}_{g}\circ\eta)\cdot(G\theta\circ\phi_{r})\big)\circ\lambda^{-1}

\displaystyle=\big((\eta\circ 1)\cdot(G\theta\circ\phi_{r})\big)\circ\lambda^{-1}

Now, we note that
\phi_{1_{x}}\circ\unit^{F}_{x}=\unit^{G}_{x}\circ 1_{\phi_{x}}
and
\phi_{s\cdot r}\circ\mult^{F}=\mult^{G}\circ(\phi_{s}\cdot\phi_{r})
.
We shall deduce that the coherence diagrams for
\phi^{*}
commute by taking
the mates of these commutative squares, thereby confirming that
\phi^{*}

is a lax horizontal transformation. Via
(i)
, we will prove
that the following pairs

\displaystyle\mult^{G}\circ(\phi_{s}\cdot\phi_{r})\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad(\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{G})\circ\alpha\circ(\phi^{*}_{s}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\phi^{*}_{r})\circ\alpha

\displaystyle\phi_{s\cdot r}\circ\mult^{F}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s\cdot r}\circ(\mult^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\phi_{1_{x}}\circ\unit^{F}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{1_{x}}\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\unit^{G}\circ 1_{\phi_{x}}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad(\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{G})\circ\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda

are under mate correspondence. The last three are one-liners, respectively:

\displaystyle\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s\cdot r}\circ(\mult^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\rho\circ(\phi_{s\cdot r}\cdot\varepsilon)\circ(\mult^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})=\rho\circ((\phi_{s\cdot r}\circ\mult^{F})\cdot\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{1_{x}}\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\rho\circ(\phi_{1_{x}}\cdot\varepsilon)\circ(\unit^{F}\cdot\mathsf{id})=\rho\circ((\phi_{1_{x}}\circ\unit^{F})\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\unit^{G})\circ\rho^{-1}\circ\lambda\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\eta)\circ\rho^{-1}

\displaystyle=\unit^{G}\circ\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\rho^{-1}\circ\eta=\unit^{G}\circ 1_{\phi_{x}}.

For the first pair, observe that

\displaystyle\lambda\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\mult^{G})\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=\lambda\circ(1\cdot\mult^{G})\circ(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=\mult^{G}\circ\lambda\circ\alpha\circ((\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle=\mult^{G}\circ(\lambda\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ((\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\cdot\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle((\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ(\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle=(((\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{s})\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle=((\gamma^{-1}\circ(\phi_{s}\cdot\varepsilon))\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\gamma^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ((\phi_{s}\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\lambda^{-1}\cdot\lambda)\circ(\phi_{s}\cdot(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}))

\displaystyle(\phi_{s}\cdot(\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id}))\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r})\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=(\phi_{s}\cdot((\varepsilon\cdot\mathsf{id})\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r}))\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=(\phi_{s}\cdot(\gamma^{-1}\circ(\phi_{r}\cdot\varepsilon)))\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}\cdot\gamma^{-1})\circ(\phi_{s}\cdot(\phi_{r}\cdot\varepsilon))\circ\alpha

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}\cdot\gamma^{-1})\circ\alpha\circ((\phi_{s}\cdot\phi_{r})\cdot\varepsilon)

and pasting the expressions above together verifies the claim.

Finally, note that

\displaystyle\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r}\circ(\mathsf{id}_{Fr}\cdot\eta_{x})

\displaystyle=(\eta_{y}\cdot\phi_{r})\circ\gamma^{-1}

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{y}\cdot\mathsf{id}_{Gr})\circ\nat^{\phi^{*}}_{r}

\displaystyle=\gamma\circ(\phi_{r}\cdot\varepsilon_{x})

are immediate consequences of mate correspondence. Thus,
\eta
and
\varepsilon
define modifications, and by calculating pointwise, we conclude
that
\phi^{*}
is the conjoint of
\phi
.
∎

We say that a vertical transformation
\phi

has a strong conjoint
(companion)
if its conjoint (companion) in the appropriate pseudodouble
category is a strong horizontal transfomation; that is, if
\nat^{\phi^{*}}

(
\nat^{\phi_{!}}
) is an invertible natural transformation. The notion of a
vertical transformation
\phi
having a strong companion (conjoint) is
present in
[
15
, A.4]
; therein, the terminology is

(co)horizontally strong
.

To provide a class of examples, recall from
[
15
, A.6]
that for a
natural transformation
\phi\colon F\to G
between pullback-preserving
functors
F\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{C}
on categories with pullbacks, the
induced vertical transformation
\hat{\phi}\colon\hat{F}\to\hat{G}

between the induced strong functors
\hat{F},\hat{G}\colon\Span(\mathcal{B})\to\Span(\mathcal{C})
has a strong conjoint if and only if it has a strong
companion, if and only if
\phi
is a cartesian natural transformation.

We also have the following the result:

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\phi\colon F\to G
be a vertical transformation of lax
functors
F,G\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
, let
H\colon\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{F}
be another lax functor. We assume
\mathbb{E}
is
conjoint closed, and that
\phi
has a strong conjoint.

H\phi
has a strong conjoint if and only if
\mult^{H}\circ(\mathsf{id}\cdot\sigma^{H})\colon HFr\cdot(H\phi_{x})^{*}\to H(Fr\cdot\phi^{*}_{x})
is
invertible for all
x
and all
r
.

Proof.

We shall verify that

(3.4)

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-35.34392pt}{28.18837pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.89027pt}{-33.22267pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.17363pt}{-4.75543pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
